WOMEN OF THE IRON 
AGE, 


—_— 


LOVE.OF FINERY IN OLDEN’ 
TIMES. , 


. “ Glastonbury, Sept. 15. 

From the.peaty soil of a meadow 
hard by thjs village archeologists 
are digging out the history of men 
and women “who' were 
civilised lives in Somerset 2,200, 
years and more ago. They were 
the Britons of the early Iron Age, 
amazingly versatile folk. © but 
rather timid, who .built their 
homes in the midst of lakes for 
fear of their enemies. 

From this ‘lake’ village of Meare 
they were compelled to row in 
their fog boats,“ scooped out of 


BRITAIN. — 


» 


CARDS AND COFFEE ON THE 
“WAY TO BUSINESS, 


TRAIN “ADVENTURES.” 


There are clubs and clubs—but 
probably, only on the London- 
Brighton section of the Southern 
Railway is a genuine travelling 
club conducted. _ : 
" Upon’ three ‘traing travel i host 
of business men who make Brighton 
or Hove their home.. Soon after 
eight every morning, they set .out 
for London.and before seven in 
the evening they are back again in 
Sussex. 

A Press representative who 
‘travelled on the 8.5, a,m. train was 
surprised to find how these season 
ticket holders make themselves at 
home, says 2 Home Correspondent. 
In one of the Pullman coaches. 
breakfasts: were served’ to those 
who, one suspected, had. slumbered 
too long to eat at home.’ Coffee 
was dispensed to most of the 
travellers, and: at several of the 
tables games of ‘cards were in pro- 
gress, oo 

A city man who detached himself 
from.s. game of solo whist to talk 
to ‘the Pressman, remarked—Yes, 
we enjoy ourselves. during our daily 
trips to town arid back. 

The strange thing is that though 
I have friends whom 1 meet on. the 
‘train every day, I don’t know the 
names of several of them nor their 
business, nor where they live. 


| Well-kept Secrets. - 


This’ is a club, if T may call it 
such, where no questions are invited 
and no “shop” allowed. For a 
couple of hours every day we for- 
get business trouble and domestic 
cares; it is “fon with the’ game.” 

J remember a year or two ago we 
had “a neweomer to our little 
“school.” a pleasant, unassuming 
fellow who lost probably every game 
rda he played. Then one day 

¢ trayelled'no more and it was not 
dill fongvafter that I learnt his 
jdentity. | He is one of the leading 

ot’ this country. His 

No, 1 cannot tell you. It 

is one'of the unwritten:rules of 

our “club” to keep our secrets with- 
in the train. * _ 

Tcortainly think the number of 
daily travellers between London 
and Brighton is increasing, you see, 
it is‘only just on hour’s run in com: 
fort and at-the end of the day's 
work there is scent of the sea. and 
the’ heights of the South Downs. 


of 


That is rather better;than 30.or, 401. 
‘and tiim|.. 


minutes in a tube trai 
suburbia when the day is over. 
Not: all: the regular-travellers go 
by Pullman coach, «Into the third 
‘class carriages, the club spirit” has 
also permeated. One foursome, 
by a little ingenuity, has made 
card-playing easy. Someone—per; 
haps,one of their wives—has made 
for them @ canvas “table.” This 
is. how it works—at each of the 
four corners is attached a tape, the 
other end of which goes over a 
player’s jacket button. When the 
journey is ended, the tapes are un- 
buttoned, and the “table” rolled 
away into an attache case. 


A Club of Three. 


The Pressman who also discover- 
ed the most exclusive book exchange 


living }. 


trees, to what wag then the vil-[. 


lage of Glastonbury. Excavations 
which have been and Still are 


being made under the direction of |, 


Dr. A. Bulleid and Mr. H.: 5: 
Gray, curator ‘of the 
Somerset County Museum, Taun- 


ton’, and secretary of the Somer- |.’ 


Socicty, have 
resulted in important discoveries. 
These prove, for instance, that 
British women of 200 B.C.. were 
just as anxious to -make the most, 
of their looks as are the women 

of to-day. . - 
Is It Rouge? - of 


Not only have all manner of 
beautiful ornaments and some use-, 
ful articles, such as bronze mirrors 
and combs, been found, but there 
have also come to light expertly 
fashioned tweezers and even a 
substance aimilar to rouge. " 

Women then had, too, a definite 
feminine love of finery, ‘a8 wit- 
neas the beautiful coloured beads 
and the many decorative and ser- 
yiceable dress pina which have 
been unearthed. —_ 

As for the men, ‘there is proof 
that they gambled, for among the 
many interesting finds are oblong- 


shaped dice, cut out of bone and ° 


‘numbered ‘in dote-“3, 4, 5,and 6, 
It estiniated that there were 120 


-housés in-the village and most of 
these have to be located.

_ THE MAN WHO". 
“WALKED WITH KINGS.” 
MR. GUSTAF ROOS 89 LAST 
MONTH. | 
_ Mr. Gustaf Roos, who’ forty 
years ago was. well known in 
every Court of Europe as the in- 
troducer of the Nordenfelt guns, 


eolebrated his 89th: birthday 
London last month. . 


in 


"T am ‘left entirely alone now" 
he told a Press representative. 


“My younger son was killed on the}. 


Somme in 1916, serving with ‘the 
York ‘and ‘Lancaster Regiment, 
and my ,elder gon, Sir George 
Roos Keppel, who was_ military 
governor of the North-West Fron< 
tler Provinces, died five years 
ago. He was said to be the only 
man Who could keep the tribes in 
order during the war. They both 
died unmarried. . a 


. “My late wife and-I celebrated 
pur-diamond wedding.in 1925, but 
she.dled last year when-we had 
been married sixty-one. years 
My sons were, “ofwcourse, of 
British nationality. I. have _re- 
tained my. Swedish nationality, 
but I came to England sixty-one 
years ago, so I am as much Eng- 
lish as Swedish, " 


“T have travelled in every cow i- 
try of Europe in connexion with 
tha. introduction of Thorsten Nor- 
denfelt’s guns, and ‘between 1889 
and 1900 I knew twelve Sovereigns, 
including the Kings of Portugal, 
Spain, Italy, Greece, Rumania, 
Denmark, the Emperor 
Francis Joseph, the old German 
Emperor, William I., Alexander 
II. of Russia,‘and the Sultan of 
Turkey. | 0 


“They all wanted me. to explain 
my guns to them, and many <a 
time I have had a longer conver- 
sation with seme ‘monarch than 
any diplomat whe only bows and 
nasses on. 


“T met King Edward only once, 
but T had the honour: of, dancing 
a cotillion with Queen ‘Alexandra 
ata ball given to her by Scandina- 
vians in Lendon “about the time 
of her wedding - 


. m 
“My son took’ King George up the

RARE ORCHIDS SOLD. 
THREE PLANTS FOR £945. 


so London; Sept. 15. 

Three — insignificant ~ looking 
planta.with long pale. green leaves, 
which Yesembled poor specimens 
of, the common. aspidistra, were 


ittle under £1,000. 

These plants, which would have 
attracted no attention on a haw- 
kers’ barrow, were very rare and 
Valuable ‘orchids—ttie Odonto- 
whith 
is said to be the finest Odonto- 


They are the gems of the col- 
lection of orehids which’ wis 
formed and cultivated for many 
years by the late Mr. H. T. Pitt, 
at Rosslyn, Stamford-hill, N., and 
is being sold this week by auction 
in.the grounds of Rosslyn. 

The Purple Emperor. bears 
‘Nowers, which measure more than 
five inches, across, of intense 
crimson-maroon,,, margined — with 
white and with white and yellow 
markings on the lip: ; 

Mr James McBean, of Cooks- 
bridge, near Lewes, Sussex, se-. 
cured the first plant for £357 and 
the second for ‘£825 10s., while 
Mr. T, Armstrong, of Tunbridge 
Wells, bought the third for £262 
108." ; 

Both the growers informed ° 
press yeporter «that they” had 
bought the orchids for themselves, 
and would probably . propagate 
from them: ; 

‘A son of the late Mr. Pitt stated 
that his father had bought . the 
plants some two years ago fer 
about £420. . my

CHINA’S NEW WAR. - 


It was disclosed in telegrams 
received yesterday that the mis- 
gion which recently went from 
Nanking to Hankow to make peace 
with General Tany Seng-chi nrov- 
ed abortive, and that the Nanking 


“s Nationalists ure definitely going 


to make war against Hadkow. for 
the purpose of subjugating this 


‘intractable section of the party, 


The sensatlonal ‘change , in’ the 
plans: of the Nationalists in ton- 
nexion with their, promised drive 
‘against (he army, of Marshal Sun 


Chuan-fany becomes explained, 


/for Nanking is out to put all ity, 


strength into the fight wit} Han- 
kow and will leave the North alone 
—for the. moment at any rate. 
What will be the outcome of this 
battle of the Yangtsze is not easy 


| to” forecast, ‘but, if would appear 


that the odds are on’ the’ Nanking 


| group, on paper at least. General 


“Tang Seng-chi and his 
federates at Hankow’ have been 
playing alone game of extremism, 
and their break with Nanking is 
largely ‘on account of the “mo- 
derate’ complexion of the latter 
party. Labour largely dominates 
the Hankow party's policy, and it 
‘looks as if} it were riding for a 
“tall.” In looking at the military 


{ position one hag to remember thit 


Hankow has a hostile force fur- 
ther up the river_and that as Nan- 
king attacks from ‘the east (and 
Nanking,is already said to be in 
control of Wuhu) the plight of the 
Wuhan forces will become seri- 
ous, having ‘inevitable repercus- 
sions in the panicky centre of the 
party’s influence. It has to be 
stdted that General Tang Seng- 
chi is an extremely capable com- 
mander and that he has many well- 
trdined troops under him, but an 
assessment of the forees now. op- 
posed to each other discloses that 
those of Nanking are the strongen/ 

And meanwhile, there is fight- 
ing going on between the Pekin 
and Shansi forces, and there :s 
Marshal Feng Yu-hsiang hovering 
around on the borders of Shansi 
awaiting. his turn to profit. “He 
may possibly go to the help of 
General Tang, but the so-called 
“Christian” General only plays a 
self-bénefitting ‘game, apparently. 
Canton does not seem minded ‘to 
Nsten to elther Nanking or Han- 
kow,|and, in the opinion of compe- 
tent observers who have closely 
followed the “Red” and anti-“Red” 


faction fights of the past week, is 


TELEGRAPH, 


| cognisnbly split up into factions: 


con-. 


on the eve of a big upheaval,’ Tho 
Nationalist party is almost unre- 


oither openly antagonistic or indif-: 
ferent to each other and wherever 
one léoks. in Chinn to-day thero 


is the same tale ofsdisintegration 
‘] and friction to be told. 


The coun- 
try was never more torn between 


itself and never was tearing less 


justified from the national stand- 
point. Of lofty sim and purpose 
there is but little and all the fight- 
ing when analysed becomes just 
so much personal wrangling and 
the disputes of éliqyes. 
haps ‘Nankitig does, try to .repre- 
sent the aims of those Nagfonalists 
who really would like to sec some- 
thing worth while ateomplished 
for the benefit of Ching ‘as a 
whole, but Nanking . has been 
rather badly. directed and has 
landed itself into the ‘maclstrom 
of fuetional intrigue without at- 
‘tracting the loyalty of those whose 
help is essential for success. It is 


| certain that the great trade of the 


Yangtsze is to be onee again in-, 
terrupted and jeopardised . and 


the Chinese people themselves will 
be the worse sufferers.

prior to the receipt of: news that 
the Nanking forees had gained an 
initial suecess. against Gen. Tang 
Seng-chi, who is reported to be 
suing for peace.| *

' The Navy Intervenes. 


The intervention ‘by a British 
submarine in Bias Bay on ‘Thursday 
night, whereby a pirated vessel was 
stopped, disabled, and the plans of 
the pirates’-an  poard frustrated, 
makes ‘thrilling reading, for one 
can imagine, apart from the official 
details, the dramatic surprise, thie 
warning shots, the ‘shell, crashing 
into the engine-room, the, blazing 
ship, the panic aboard when scores 
of people jumped. into the water, 
and the great efforts of the sub- 
marine crew in effecting, reseues 
under the utmost diffieulties’ of 
darkneds and a rough sea.- It pro- 
vides material for the: vivid 
imagination of a film scenario 
writer, and hardly conforms 
to . the prosaic work of 
a naval vesel on the’ China 
“coast. Huwever, it is faet, and 
adds another chapter to the history 
of the. British Nayy's activities in 
various waters of the wide world. 
Also, it goes further, in emphasis- 
ing the cifcctiveness-of the pre- 
sence of British warships in the 
Bias Bay vicinity, in checking the 
activities of piratical gangs.) The 
capture of the Irene followed the 
usual programme of disguised pas- 
sengers, and had the’ Ld not been 
where she was on Thursday night,, 
the tale would most probably have 
been one of looting, kidnapping, and 
a vessel stdaming into port, after 
bping overdue ut her "destination, 
with a tale of another point for the 
Bias Bay score shect.. 
the plans of the desperadves have 
gone all awry, as badly as in the 
case ‘of the s.s. Sunning. The 
{prompt foiling of the seheme for 
the seizura of the Irene will teach 
its lasting lesson. Jt is unfertun- 
ate that there hus been some unin. 
tended loss of life, but that appears 
inevitable under the circumstances 
of the case. The heroic resctie work 
by the submarine’s’ crew: speaks 
again of the fine traditions’ of the 
Service, nobly .fulfilled. At Blas 
Bay, and elsewhere, the affair sill 
serve as & much-needed warning as’ 
to what, pirates ‘can expect in the 
event of outrages on vessels on the 
high seas. Never again will a 
course be set for the bay by a free- 
booter gang without the fear that 
somewhere, ready tu appear at any 
‘moment, there may be lurking one 
of His Majesty’s efficient policemen 
Of the ocean mo

Tyre Danger. 


. It has been definitely established 
that the rear tyres are the first 
do wear out, and’ many motorists 
shift the rear dyres to the front 
wheels in order to" even up the 
wear, ("Only this week, we met 
with an instance of this almost 
general practice, and would utter 
a wort of warning. against the 
[danger created. When a worn 
tyre blows out @n-a rear wheel, 
while the ear is travelling atoa 
hich rate,of speed, the driver still 
has cnough control of the front

‘UNIQUE CLUTCH.

Noiseless Gear Change. 


—— tt 


OPTIONAL FREE- 
WHEEL. 


Several types of free-whee! clutch 
are gaining attention in Britain, 
und some manufacturers are experi- 
menting with them. ° ; . 


From reports made by engineers, 


the most promising seems to be the | 


Humtrey-Sandberg design, which 
makes gear-chanying foolproof, and 
yet can .cease to be a free-wheel 
clutch at the driver’s option. "4 
Freewaecling may save: petrol; 
but it would certainly result in the 


greates use of brakes, and, on tony | ° 


steep hills, where an engine-in ‘low 
gear is the best possible brake, pos- 
sibly allow the car to pile up ina 
heup at the bottom; for when it is 
in operation, the clutch. disengages 
as soon ag the ear overruns the en- 
gine, and that is a debatable ad- 
vyaulage, : 

The Humrrey-Sandberg has been 
developed froma scheme of  con- 


_ elutehes, invented, origin- 
y, with idea of making gear- 
hane wsisy and ndiseless. 

- That, aceording to reports, has 
now been achieved in a simpler man- 
ner, “und on a car filted with 
this device, if is possible to change 
fram.top. to bettom, -or -vice-versa, 
without 2 sound, and the clutch does 
not re-enzage until the revolutions 
of the engine approximate to the 
speed of the car. ; 

‘This cluteh is contained in a quite 
small. casing, fitted belween sgear- 
box and torque tube, and, by"a’small 
lever fitted beside the gear-shift, 
the clutch can be locked. This must 
be done when ‘reversing; and the 
same action would allow the engine 
to act as a brake on a-steep hill, 
while the car,‘could be allowed to

pool’ in three consecutive meet-

Cleaning Out Dust. 
rn cn 

f : . 

RENEWING OIL. 
. (By Israel Klein.) 

On the return from a long trip |, 
the cay, like the rest of us, needs 
2 general cleaning and overhauling. 

The road is nene too kind to any 
automobile; especially after several 
days of constant touring. without a. 
minute’s — attention. The body 
xquenks, the chassis frame is looge, 
the engine is noisy and the car it- 
self full of dust amd-grime. 

it is therefore goad prattice not 
only to have the body cleaned, but. 
to. chéck over all running parts, to’ 
tighten jup all body and chassis 
bults, and see that all joints are 
well greased, . 

Dust gets into the cracks and 
openings of the automobile, and ac-' 
cumulates to a startling degree on 
ony sort of journey. ‘The dry. dirt 
roids we meet on almost any trip 
throw, up great quantities of ‘thin 
‘dust that creeps into the crankcase, 
into the transmission housing and 
into the carburetor. 

This dust must be eleated out. 
The oil in the crankcase should be 
changed and before new oil is put 
ina quart of thin fresh oi] should 
be used to flush it out. : 

New heavy oil or grease should 
be put into the’ transmission and 
rear housing, especially if the e&r 
has been run a few thousand miles 
without a change. : 

To be sure of a thorough cleans- 
ing, the, engine also should, be 
washed with) gasoline ‘or. under 
steam pressure. _— 

Sometimes mud, thrown | back |" 
fram: bumping through wet roads, 
clogs up the air vents-of the radja- 
tor, reducing its cooling efficiency. 

The radiator, therefore; should be 
washed thoroughly | after ‘a long 
In doing so, it is advisable 
to cover the front of the engine, 
especially the’ eleétrical system, with 
an oilcloth or rubbér coat, to’ pre- 
vent water spraying over it: and 
rendering. it ineffective. . 

If the car. has gone, over long 
nmeuntiin roads, the brakes need 
vareful adjustment, and perhaps re- 
lining, . 

Another mountain climbing after- 
effect is the softening of the hose 
vouplings in the. water cooling: sys- 
tem, sfrom overheating of the en- 
gine. . These should be checked up 
and, if they are dracked or show 
considerable wear, should be re- 
placed with new couplings. o 

Overheating also ‘takes large 
chunks. of rubber from these cou- 
plings arid mixes thenrinto. the cool-, 
ing system. These chunks might 
clog up the radiator. The whole 
system therefore should be drained 
and flushed, several times, before 
fresh witer is put in. . 

After the cleaning, the chassis 
joints should bé greased thoroupea- 
ly, and the springs should not be 
overlooked. : 

The vbody, too, needs tightening 
to keep the car in guod running 
‘condition forthe rest of the year,

Running Costs Records, 


The business ,of — delivering 
paxeels’ by motor vin has during 
the past few years made tremendous 
-strides, In this class of 
the question of .running ‘costs is 
extremely important, for the whole 
basis of profit’ is simply: the dif- 
ference between the charge for de- 
livering the parcels and -the all-in 
cost of running a flect of vehicles. 

Where every iwvehicle in a large 
fleet is covering fifteen to twenty 
thousand miles per annum, a dit- 
ference of even a farthing per mile 
in running casts may easily make 
thousands of pounds difference in 
the annual profits. : 

in.fact,’sucha minute saving per 
mile run may make all the difference 
between -profit and loss at ,the end 
of the year. Needless to‘say, while 
an accurate check on running costs 
Lis essential for any user of motor 
transport, this is absblutcly vital 
in a caso such ag this.

well known firm, Messrs, Carter. 
Paterson and Co., of London, who 
have now -many hundrods of 
vehicles In active service."

ly increasing to cope with the: do- 
mand for their serviecs. Naturally 
a concern such as this has a vast 
experiance of different commerical 
vehicles, and it ig Interesting to 
note that. they recently, placed ‘an 
order with the Albion Motor Car 
Co., of Glasgow, for thirteen $0 
ewt, chassis, . ‘

Carter, Paterson must now be run-. 
ning well on for 200 of this popular 
machine, which -is o pretty. good in- 
ication of their ‘economical run-

Know the Width of Your’ Car, 

We have been observing very, 
closely during recent journeys the 
driving: of those motorists) “who 


may be' judged by many of their | 


actions te be in the novice category, 


remurks The Motor. . : 
One of their chief failings, we 


are convinced, is a total inability to- 


gauge the width of their. vehicles. 
Over and over again it will be 
found that drivers of this type are 
in a state of nerves lest they should 
drive too near to the kerb. 

Their judgment in this respect 
when they are passing vehicles 
coming towards them is irvarinbly 
at fault, and what to the driver 
of ‘another vehicle appears to be 
stubborn reluctance to give way and 
allow safe passing room is _ more

Tyre Wear and Woman Driver. 
Many unkind things have. been 
said about the carelessness of the 
woman driver, but while sha may 
be carcless on the roads she pro- 
bably. takes more care of her car, 
than the average man does. . 
Tho vesult is a detrease | in 
running expenses, and the mainten- 
ance of the cnr’s tune and tone over 


lvery long periods.’ 


An example of this is shown by 
an English girl owner who averaged « 
over 10,000 miles per annum on & 
‘Fourteen-Thirty hp. Armstrong 


Siddeley for the last two years, and 


whose tyres have covered 17,200 
miles, 19,000 miles, 19,987 and 
nenriy 21,000 miles in the period. 
While these figures speak ‘well . 
for the general tyre economy ofthe 
ear, they also indicate the splendid ; 
results that can be obtained by a.” 
little care on the part of the owner ~ 
to maintain her tyres at their 
correct pressure. .

Excessive Oil Consumption, - 

Questiun—The compression of 
my engine is good, built it uses 
too much oil. Will one -’ oil-ring, 
installed’ upon ‘each — piston, 
this of] losg or should two or used? 

Answer—lIf you are certain that 
an’ excessive amount of oil passes 
the pistons, ‘an ‘oil-control ring, 
properly installed in’ the “bottom 
an effective remedy.’ If there is an 
pistons, we should expect that the 
exhaust would be smoky, that the 
plugs would foul badly dnd that the 
piston-heads would’ collect carbon 
very rapidly. . Unless: .you knew 
thaub some of these conditions exist, 
it may be that the loss of oil 
not past the piston to any abnormal 
degree, but that it is through ex- 
ternal leaks, 
control rings, make sure that there 
is no leakage out of the crankcage, 
timing-gear or value:compartment, 
Spread a clean paper under the 


engine, and run it idle ‘for. 15 or 20]: 


minutes at a 20 m. p, h. speed and 
see if there is any oil! dropped. on 
it. If there is have the leaks stop- 
ped, but if there is not, try the oil 
rings after getting your’factory to 
recommend what kind to use.

‘TO GIVE! UP.

“London, Oct.. 21. 
The London typist, Miss Gleitze, 
started from Cap Grisnez at 4.28 
this morning .in an attempt to 
repeat her Channel swim in view o 
Dr: Dorothy Logan's hoax. : 


" A score of people are accompany- 
ing the swimmer ina boat, includ- 
ing Pressmon and two doctors 
brought, by Mr. Sidney Hirst, the 
seeretary of the .Amateur Swim- 
ming Club who stated that it was 
imperative for a medical: man to 
be present to judge when the limit 
was reached as the ordeal was very 
dangerous owing to the coldness of 
the water.--Renuter. 


[A message dated the 1Gth an- 
nounced ‘that a sensatiorial state- 
ment had‘been made by the Neies of 
the World that the Channel swim 
alleged to hive been accomplished 
by "Mona 9 MeLennan,’”” (Dr. 
Darethy Logan) on October 10 was 
n hoax carried oat by Dr. Logan in 
order to'show the desirability of 
an International Commission to 
control, supervise and certify all 
Channel swims.] "

the man disappeared, and all

proved fruitless. .

The University Union was 
crowded Inst evening when Miss 
Wong Sui-Jan, of the Ying Wah 
Girls’ School, won the Ladies’ 
Singles Championship by defeat- 
ing. Miss Rose Kwok, of the Hong- 
kong University, in 8 sets to 2, 6-3, 
10-8, 6-8, 3-6,°8-6." It wag a fiard 
struggle throughout and both 
players were in very ‘good form, 
Miss Kwok gave a, splendid ex- 
hibition of. forehand‘ drives in the 
3rd cand dth sets, but playing a 
sound defensive game Miss Wong 

won the final set and the match:

A team of the Marylebone Cric- 
kel Club left England this morn- 
ing for South Africa. ee :

“Tt.ig a strong team; and we dre 
preity confident of putting up a 
sood fight. "The team ts Strong in 
batting, and well-equipped with 
spin bowlers. We ‘have 
matches to play, including — five 
Test matches, and I think we 
‘should, win the ‘rubher.—British

The resolutions passed by the 
‘Econothic «Conference at Geneva 
concerning the causes of economic 
depression» in mdst European’ 
States and the removal of these 
ciuses constitute .a victory for tha 
evolutionists, Le., for all those who |: 
helleve in the necessity ‘and pos- 
sibility of all -civilized countries 
systematically co-operating in]: 
certain economic spheres witho it 
destroying the sovereignty of the |, 
individual States, writes one who fp. - 
took part in it,"in an ‘exchange. | 
Tt ia highly significant that these |} 
resolutions: wore adopted by 2 
gathering which, in composition. |. 
could searcely have been’ more |. 
heterogeneous, not to say an-|- 
tagonistic; it represented capita- 
lism and ‘bolshevism, protection |’ 
und free trade, employers and.em- |. 
ployeds, commerce, manufacture | | 
and agriculture. . ’ : 

Anyone acquainted with the op- 
posing — interests existing éven 
within thé various groups them- 
selves will appreciate, the work 
achieved by the League of "Na- 
tions in harmonizing,. to auch a 
‘degreé, all these conflicting in-|- 
terests. ‘Those who had an on-|’ 
portunity of observing how, de- 
spite the protectionist policy . of | 
their Government, the French de- [2% ” 
‘Jegates did all in their power, to 
prevent the Conference from tom- 
ing to gric#. in. consequence of 
their country’s attitude must sze |. 
thersin a striking practical ‘ ¢x- 
ample of the intense desire , felt: 
everywhere to-day, for: the pre- |’ 
servation of international -soli- 
darity. It is greatly to the credit 1-3 
of the League that it has, ‘by 
means of the Economic Confer- 
ence, confirmed: this solidarity |: 
and the necessity for international 
co-operation. 

Supported by the authority of 
this assembly, the idea of co- 
operation and of a general balanc- | 
ing of international economic in- 
terests will gain ground in,all

UNWARY. : 

ou. It generally assumed that it is 
natural for a child to be afraid of 
the dark, of thunder, and sudden 
noises and that the child must be 
lnughed, out of such fears or even 
punished for them. 

_ Inherited Fears. 

Nothing could be more wrong and 
cruel, childish fears are not natural 
and are due in the main to inherit- 
ance or wrong -upbringing, and in 
some cases to experience. A great 
deal ‘is due to prenatal influences. 
It often happens that if the mother 
has been frightened by a.storm or 
a dog, for instance, before the birth 
of her child, the child will show

Saltpetre. 


It is better to use rain water 
than hard tap water for flowers, 
and it should be easy to do so here 
with our regulation inch, every 
three days. But if you‘ canno 
obtain it, there are a number of 
substances which will help, when |, 
dissolved in their water to keep 
flowers fresh. A pinch of salt, a 
lump of washing soda, a lump of 
sugar, all have thelr champions, 
but’ I have found a pinch. of salt |: 
petre, which contains nitrogen, or 

-/a tablet of- aspirin are the best. ° 


Daily Care. - 


The water in which flowera are 
kept-should be changed daily. At 
the same time about a quarter of 
an inch should be out from the end 
of cach stalk, beeause when a 
flower is cut the sap oozes out-and |- 
seals’ the end of the stalk thus 
preventing the drawing up of 
nourishment. Flowers need plenty 
of fresh air and soon die if kept 
in a stuffy room, and it is always 
wiser to keép them away from the 
dust rays of any artificial light.

Crows’ feet, those fine wrinkles around the eyes, are often 
the first sign of age. ‘ : 

They are caused not so much. by years as by too dry a 
akin, one that lacks both oil'and moisture. Drink from eight 
to ten glasses of water daily and use a good rich skin food 
twice a day to eliminate them. . : ‘ 

Massage is the most helpful way to eradicate them. Dip 
the tips’of your fingers in muscle oil, or sweet oil if you lack 
the prepared lotion, and beginning at the outside corner of 
the eye, massage-very, very gently under the eye towards the 
nose, rounding upwards and making a circular motion over the . 
eyeball towards the outside of the cye again. ‘Lift fingers from 
eye and begin again. 

Massage cach eye ten times." 
sides of the eye where the wrinkles appear. 
‘ on all night. :

A Flrengn's Bravery. 

It is said that while the —sub- 
marine were firing at the Irena 
the pirates were all-in a panic 
and one of them got down into 
the engine’ room and .asked' the 
firemen to steam'up to prevent 
capture. Later when the fireman 
heard the firing of the guns 
becoming  fiercer every minute, 
and when he sav the pirate climb- 
ing the stairs up to the’ upper 
deck he pulled him * down 
again and struck him a blow which 
sent him into the burning fire. 
The fireman then made his escape 
and it is believed the freebooter 
must have met his. death in th
